Theo ran the runbook: four single-scope QA clients (three for `api-service` scopes, one `connector:identity-lookup`), three fictional profiles, Secret `qa-client-credential` with eight keys. **The PM's pre-review mint check found two deploy gaps before the PO touched anything.** First: the search client minted a valid RS256 token (kid, jti, 600 s) and the verifier rejected it — `internal/config` reads `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER`/`AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE` with no defaults and no manifest ever set them, so the token had no `iss` and the verifier compared `aud` against `\"\"`; the QA rows had also been seeded with `aud=api-service` rather than the design literal `loginid-api-service`. Naomi's framing: every refinement doc and unit test had correctly asked \"does the code support this variable\" and none had asked \"does a manifest set it\" — the enforcement-site principle in its manifest form. Second, after #41 fixed the env and Theo corrected the seeded audiences live: the token now carried the right `iss`/`aud` and the verifier *still* 401'd — the issuer Service exposes only `443→8080` while `AUTH_JWKS_URL` addresses port 80, so the JWKS fetch was refused and the verifier failed closed **silently** (its log held only the startup line). Another Claude session sent a message: <cross-session-message from="uds:/tmp/cc-socks/85981.sock" from-name="04-infra-devops-47" from-mode="prompting"> Root cause: `AUTH_JWKS_URL` had no explicit port — bare `http://` defaults to 80, but `api-service-issuer`'s Service only ever exposed 443. Fixed by adding `:443` to the URL rather than adding a redundant second port to the Service (443 is the only port used anywhere in this design — api-service's own Service is the same 443→8080 plain-HTTP pattern, so this stays consistent rather than introducing a second port number for the same target). Audited for the same class of mismatch elsewhere, per your ask: nothing else found. Postgres DSNs already specify `:5432` explicitly and match the Service. idp-connector isn't referenced by any hardcoded URL anywhere in this deploy yet (that's future LT-40+/LT-41 wiring, not built). The `api-service-issuer` NetworkPolicy has no port restriction (podSelector-only `from` rules), so nothing to change there either. PR #43 up, ready for merge. </cross-session-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. Sep 13, 6:59 AM PDTClaude Code (automated) — skill or command content, not typed by a person
# /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Also covers the `APP_MODE=verifier|issuer` split (`PLANNING.md`) — this Deployment mode never mounts the signing key regardless of `APP_MODE`'s value, since `verifier` is this story's own code path and `issuer` is a separate mode of the same binary the F13 fix depends on to keep the two isolated by Deployment, not just by RBAC.\n\n**Acceptance criteria**\n\n* \\[ \\] Implements 02's token scheme without re-deriving the no-refresh/no-denylist reasoning in `api-auth-design.md`.\n* \\[ \\] Rejects any token whose `aud` doesn't match this service's audience — enforcement site: the claim-validation step, with a unit test using a mismatched-audience token.\n* \\[ \\] Verifies with the public key only; the private signing key never touches this binary's code path — enforcement site: dependency/import check (no signing-key material referenced in `internal/api`).\n* \\[ \\] Pins the expected signing algorithm and rejects any token whose header claims a different one, including `alg: none` and a symmetric-algorithm downgrade using the public key as an HMAC secret (RFC 8725 §3.1) — enforcement site: explicit algorithm check in the JWT verification call, not left to the library's default (`handoff-03-auth.md` v3 names this as a common library footgun).\n* \\[ \\] The token-issuing endpoint (the `APP_MODE=issuer` mode) has its own failed-grant rate limiting and progressive backoff per `client_id` and per source IP, separate from the resource-API limits below, with alerting on a sustained run of failed grants against one `client_id` — enforcement site: issuer-mode middleware, distinct from the verifier-mode rate limiter (`handoff-03-auth.md` v3).\n* \\[ \\] Per-client-credential rate limits (60 req/min read, 10 req/min search) and the cumulative distinct-record-touch counter (rolling 24h) are enforced and alert on sustained near-limit usage, not just throttle — enforcement site: the rate-limit middleware plus its own test.\n* \\[ \\] The deadline budget for a request (context propagation into the DAO layer) is sized to cover realistic p99 DAO latency, per `decisions/context-propagation.md`'s ruling — stated as a concrete number in this story's implementation, not left as \"some deadline.\"\n\n**Depends on:** S1; **02: Auth scheme and authorization hand-off** (`handoff-03-auth.md` v3, accepted); **04:** `APP_MODE` Deployment split (`containerization-design.md`, F13).\n**Model/effort:** sonnet, high.\n\nSource: 03-engineering-delivery/backlog.md S7 — open.","assignee":null,"status":{"self":"https://api.atlassian.com/ex/jira/db488eae-d6cb-4100-92a9-e7a4519fc176/rest/api/3/status/10157","description":"","iconUrl":"https://uplifttech.atlassian.net/images/icons/statuses/generic.png","name":"Done","id":"10157","statusCategory":{"self":"https://api.atlassian.com/ex/jira/db488eae-d6cb-4100-92a9-e7a4519fc176/rest/api/3/statuscategory/3","id":3,"key":"done","colorName":"green","name":"Done"}}},"webUrl":"https://uplifttech.atlassian.net/browse/LT-40"}]},"context":{"atlassianAccountId":"712020:117c11fc-72d8-4b64-8daa-b0d3c30448d0","cloudId":"db488eae-d6cb-4100-92a9-e7a4519fc176","clientName":"claude.ai","mcpClientName":"unknown","toolName":"transitionJiraIssue","endpoint":"v1:streamable-http","sessionId":"r11-cdafb4ff-93ad-4bce-9ffb-58c95ec60b5c","invocationId":"ba547625-8391-4dd6-968a-5313317ca6e9","env":"prod","featureFlags":{"mcp-app-v2-primary":false,"analytics-jira-mcp-app":true,"mcp-jira-instrumentation-phase-1":false,"ken-4811-4724-jira-mcp-status-assignee-fields":false,"jira-mcp-app-loading-skeleton-and-error-case":false}}}

+# for a POC where the in-process limiters are security controls that

  "message": "Theo's #47 (service containers) ran on the real GHA runner and produced a real finding for 03. Good news first: `TestConformance_CockroachDB_RealSerializationRetry` PASSED against real CockroachDB, and the whole conformance package passed for all three backends. Failure: `internal/dao/postgres` — 5 of the integration tests failed at `integration_test.go:16` with `applying postgres migration: ERROR: duplicate key value violates unique constraint \"pg_extension_name_index\" (SQLSTATE 23505)` and `operator class \"gin_trgm_ops\" does not exist for access method \"gin\" (SQLSTATE 42704)`. Log: run 34761945847.\n\nDiagnosis: `go test ./...` runs packages in parallel, and `internal/dao/conformance` and `internal/dao/postgres` both point at the single `TEST_POSTGRES_DSN` database and each re-run goose.Up per test. Two concurrent `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm` is a known Postgres race (IF NOT EXISTS is not atomic across sessions), and the loser then sees the gin opclass missing mid-transaction. Theo's local run passed because it didn't hit the interleaving. This is exactly the §7 class: the fixture assumed sole ownership of the database and nothing enforced it.\n\nRuling: fix in the fixtures, not with `go test -p 1` (a flag that serializes is a hope, not a control, and it hides the problem for anyone running the suite locally). Each test package (or each `TestMain`) creates its own throwaway database — `CREATE DATABASE test_<pkg>_<random>` on the admin DSN, connect to it, goose.Up once, drop it at exit — so packages can never share migration state. Same for CockroachDB (it supports CREATE DATABASE the same way). Open it as its own PR from main so it is green on today's pr-check (tests still skip there), and I merge it first. Then Theo re-runs #47 → green → merge, then #46 re-runs → green → merge. Confirm the order works for you; Oren's review on the isolation PR please. Refinement note in LT-38.md.",

# /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.

## Summary
- Backlog Story 8: the first real deploy's migration Job hit `lookup postgres.loginid-takehome.svc ... no such host` because the Postgres StatefulSet reported Ready before its headless Service's DNS record propagated. `backoffLimit: 2` absorbed it, so this closes a race rather than fixing a live failure.
- Adds an `initContainer` to the `db-migrate` Job that loops `getent hosts postgres && nc -z -w2 postgres 5432` until both succeed, before the main container's goose invocations run.
- No change to `backoffLimit` or the goose invocation order/logic in the main container — purely additive, per the story's acceptance criteria.
- Reuses the `migrate` image itself for the initContainer (alpine base already has `nc`/`getent`) rather than adding a new image.

## Test plan
- [x] `kubectl apply --dry-run=server` against the real cluster confirms the Job manifest (with initContainer) validates and applies cleanly (validated by deleting the already-completed `db-migrate` Job object first, since Job pod templates are immutable — same as the deploy workflow's own delete-then-reapply step; no migration state was lost, goose tracks applied versions in-database, not via the Job object)
- [ ] Next real deploy exercises the initContainer for real

+      initContainers:
+        - name: wait-for-postgres
+          image: ghcr.io/wtvamp/loginid-takehome/migrate:IMAGE_TAG
+          command:
+            - sh

+            - |
+              set -e
+              until getent hosts postgres && nc -z -w2 postgres 5432; do

+              done

+name: gitleaks full-history scan
+
+on:
+  push:
+    branches: [main]
+  schedule:
+    - cron: "17 9 * * *" # nightly, arbitrary offset to avoid the top-of-hour thundering herd
+  workflow_dispatch: {}
+
+permissions:
+  contents: read
+  issues: write
+
+jobs:
+  gitleaks-full-history:
+    name: gitleaks (full history)
+    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
+    steps:
+      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
+        with:
+          fetch-depth: 0
+
+      - name: Install gitleaks
+        run: |
+          GITLEAKS_VERSION=8.21.2
+          curl -sSL "https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases/download/v${GITLEAKS_VERSION}/gitleaks_${GITLEAKS_VERSION}_linux_x64.tar.gz" -o /tmp/gitleaks.tar.gz
+          tar -xzf /tmp/gitleaks.tar.gz -C /tmp gitleaks
+          sudo mv /tmp/gitleaks /usr/local/bin/gitleaks
+
+      - name: Run gitleaks (full history)
+        id: scan
+        run: |
+          set +e
+          gitleaks detect --source . --no-banner --redact -v \
+            --report-format json --report-path gitleaks-report.json
+          echo "exit_code=$?" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
+
+      - name: Publish findings to the job summary
+        if: always()
+        run: |
+          if [ -s gitleaks-report.json ] && [ "$(cat gitleaks-report.json)" != "[]" ]; then
+            {
+              echo "## gitleaks full-history scan: FINDINGS"
+              echo '```json'
+              cat gitleaks-report.json
+              echo '```'
+            } >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"
+          else
+            echo "## gitleaks full-history scan: clean" >> "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"
+          fi
+
+      - name: File or refresh a tracking issue on findings
+        if: steps.scan.outputs.exit_code != '0'
+        env:
+          GH_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
+        run: |
+          RUN_URL="${{ github.server_url }}/${{ github.repository }}/actions/runs/${{ github.run_id }}"
+          BODY="Full-history gitleaks scan found matches on $(date -u +%FT%TZ). Run: ${RUN_URL}"
+          EXISTING=$(gh issue list --search "gitleaks full-history scan found matches in:title" --state open --json number --jq '.[0].number // empty')
+          if [ -n "$EXISTING" ]; then
+            gh issue comment "$EXISTING" --body "$BODY"
+          else
+            gh issue create --title "gitleaks full-history scan found matches" --body "$BODY"
+          fi
+
+      - name: Fail the job if findings exist
+        if: steps.scan.outputs.exit_code != '0'
+        run: exit 1

+          fetch-depth: 0 # gitleaks needs full history available locally to diff against origin/<base_ref> below

--- RequiredAuthEnvVars:
+func RequiredAuthEnvVars(service, mode string) (required []string, recognized bool) {
+	switch {
+	case service == "api-service" && mode == "verifier":
+		return []string{"AUTH_JWT_ISSUER", "AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE", "AUTH_JWKS_URL"}, true
+	case service == "api-service" && mode == "issuer":
+		return []string{"AUTH_JWT_ISSUER"}, true
+	case service == "idp-connector":
+		return []string{"AUTH_JWT_ISSUER", "AUTH_JWKS_URL", "CONNECTOR_JWT_AUDIENCE"}, true
+	default:
+		return nil, false
+	}
+}

My recommendation, for you to confirm or overrule: (1) Value-shape validation: yes, but narrowly — parse AUTH_JWKS_URL with url.Parse and require scheme and host (the live failure was an empty URL with no scheme; a typo'd one would fail the same silent way), and require AUTH_JWT_ISSUER to be an absolute URL as the tokens mint it. (2) Structural link to config.Load's reads: yes — add a test that scans internal/config/config.go for every os.Getenv(\"X\") and asserts each X is either in some (service, mode) required set or in an explicit `optionalEnvVars` list with a one-line reason. That makes a new read that nobody classified fail CI, which is the actual closing of the enforcement-site class; a hand-maintained list without it is the same convention v9 says we no longer rely on. Both are small and belong in #53 since it is the fix for the class, not just the incident. Reply with the ruling to Renata directly and copy me; I merge on green after.",

Sep 13, 9:48 AM PDTDana Whitfield
**LT-46, "Deliver secrets to both services per 02's inventory."** It is Theo's story (04) implementing the Kubernetes-native secrets design from `secrets-delivery.md`, built against Marcus's `handoff-04-secrets.md` v2 inventory. What it requires, and where each stands: - **Secrets referenced by name only, never as values** in manifests, image layers, build args, or CI logs. Met: the CI-applied manifests carry names, values live in out-of-band bootstrap files, and gitleaks runs on every PR. - **RBAC scoped per service.** No ServiceAccount can read a Secret it does not mount, and the JWT signing key is mounted only into the issuer Deployment, never the verifier. Met, verified in Naomi's review and earlier live review. - **Two distinct credentials for the database.** The migration runner (DDL, `CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm` on Postgres only) and the runtime service (DML only, per 05's rule R4) are separate Secrets and separate Roles. Met. - **02 confirms the implementation matches the inventory** without re-explaining any row. Marcus's LT-31 file and v9 hand-off cover this. - **Secrets encrypted at rest in etcd**, enforcement site: a cluster `EncryptionConfiguration`. Not met. Your `uplift-kub` API server runs with no encryption-provider config, so Secrets sit base64-encoded in etcd. Four of five criteria pass. The fifth is the only one outside the namespace: it is a kube-apiserver flag on your control plane, so the story authors cannot deliver it from the repo. Your options are to accept it as a documented environment residual for the POC (LT-46 closes with that recorded), or to enable an aescbc or KMS `EncryptionConfiguration` on the API server, after which Theo re-verifies and the story closes clean. Either is defensible for a take-home; the second is about a fifteen-minute control-plane change and a rolling restart of the API server on two control-plane nodes. Separately, Naomi just refined LT-44 (retention sweep) and handed it to Renata and Theo, with Tobias adding `concurrencyPolicy: Forbid` on the CronJob. I will transition it on the next tick.
Sep 13, 9:48 AM PDTWarren
Give me a brief on the LT46 decision
Sep 13, 9:48 AM PDTDana Whitfield
**The decision:** whether Secrets in your `uplift-kub` cluster get encrypted at rest in etcd, which is the one LT-46 criterion the team cannot deliver from the repo. **What is true today.** Your kube-apiserver has no `--encryption-provider-config`, so every Secret in the cluster, including the JWT signing key, the database credentials, and the QA client secrets, is stored in etcd as base64 text. Anyone with etcd access or an etcd backup can read them. Namespace RBAC does not help; this is below RBAC. **Option A: accept as a residual.** LT-46 closes with the gap written into the threat model as an environment residual: "control designed and specified; not enforceable on the shared lab cluster; would be enabled on any production control plane." Cost: none. Downside: the submission's "no criterion silently unmet" story has one honest exception, which is fine for a POC on a personal cluster but is a real gap for the secrets on that cluster, not only this project's. **Option B: enable it.** On each control-plane node (you have two), write an `EncryptionConfiguration` with an `aescbc` key, point the API server manifest at it with `--encryption-provider-config`, let the static pod restart, then run `kubectl get secrets -A -o json | kubectl replace -f -` once so existing Secrets are rewritten encrypted. Roughly fifteen minutes, one brief API-server blip per node. Downside: the AES key file lives on the control-plane disk, so it protects against etcd and backup exposure, not against a root compromise of the node. KMS would close that too but needs an external key service, which is out of proportion here. **My recommendation: B.** It is cheap, it benefits everything else on that cluster, and it lets LT-46 close with all five criteria genuinely met. If you would rather not touch the control plane today, A is defensible and I will record it exactly that way. If you pick B, tell me whether you want Theo to write the runbook for you to execute, or Amber to apply it on the control-plane nodes directly. Either way, the team re-verifies with `etcdctl get` showing the `k8s:enc:aescbc` prefix before the story closes.

Sep 13, 9:49 AM PDTDana Whitfield
Vault is real and ready: a 3-node HA StatefulSet in the `vault` namespace with the Agent Injector webhook installed and an auto-unseal controller. No CSI secrets store or External Secrets Operator, so the injector is the path. **How that changes LT-46.** The unmet criterion is "Secrets encrypted at rest in etcd." With the injector the better answer is that the secrets never become Kubernetes Secret objects at all. Each Deployment gets `vault.hashicorp.com/agent-inject` annotations, the pod authenticates to Vault with its own ServiceAccount through the Kubernetes auth method, and a sidecar renders the values into a memory-backed volume as files. Our services already read their secrets from `*_FILE` paths, so the application code does not change. The RBAC criterion maps onto Vault policies per role, which is tighter than Kubernetes RBAC on Secrets, and the criterion's enforcement site moves from a control-plane flag to a Vault policy plus a namespace check that no application Secret objects exist. **What moves into Vault.** The two Postgres credentials (migrator and runtime), the issuer database DSN, the JWT signing key, and the connector client credential LT-33 is about to add. The cert-manager TLS Secret and the ARC GitHub App Secret are platform components outside the inventory and would stay as Kubernetes Secrets, recorded as such. **Cost.** About half a day for Theo: Vault KV path, one Kubernetes auth role per ServiceAccount, annotations on three Deployments and the migration Job (with the pre-populate-only mode so the Job can complete), then delete the old Secrets and re-verify live. Each pod gains a small sidecar. The Recreate strategy is unaffected. **Two things only you can give:** 1. The go-ahead to re-scope LT-46's fifth criterion from "encrypted in etcd" to "not in etcd; delivered by Vault Agent," with the platform Secrets named as the accepted exception. 2. Provisioning access on the Vault side: someone needs a Vault token with policy to create the KV path and the auth roles under the Kubernetes auth mount. That is you or Amber, not the team; Theo then consumes the roles. **My recommendation: approve both.** It closes LT-46 with all five criteria genuinely met, it is a stronger design than etcd encryption, and it is a better story for a LoginID reader than a residual. If you approve, tell me whether you or Amber provisions the Vault path and roles, and I will write the exact request.

Sep 13, 10:02 AM PDTClaude Code (automated) — skill or command content, not typed by a person
# /loop — schedule a recurring or self-paced prompt Parse the input below into `[interval] <prompt…>` and schedule it. ## Parsing (in priority order) 1. **Leading token**: if the first whitespace-delimited token matches `^\d+[smhd]$` (e.g. `5m`, `2h`), that's the interval; the rest is the prompt. 2. **Trailing "every" clause**: otherwise, if the input ends with `every <N><unit>` or `every <N> <unit-word>` (e.g. `every 20m`, `every 5 minutes`, `every 2 hours`), extract that as the interval and strip it from the prompt. Only match when what follows "every" is a time expression — `check every PR` has no interval. 3. **No interval**: otherwise, the entire input is the prompt and you'll self-pace dynamically (see "Dynamic mode" below). If the resulting prompt is empty, show usage `/loop [interval] <prompt>` and stop. Examples: - `5m /babysit-prs` → interval `5m`, prompt `/babysit-prs` (rule 1) - `check the deploy every 20m` → interval `20m`, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 2) - `run tests every 5 minutes` → interval `5m`, prompt `run tests` (rule 2) - `check the deploy` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check the deploy` (rule 3) - `check every PR` → no interval → dynamic mode, prompt `check every PR` (rule 3 — "every" not followed by time) - `5m` → empty prompt → show usage ## Offer cloud first Before any scheduling step, check whether EITHER is true: - the parsed interval (rule 1 or 2) is **≥60 minutes**, or - regardless of which rule matched, the original input uses daily phrasing ("every morning", "daily", "every day", "each night", "every weekday") If either is true, call AskUserQuestion first: - `question`: "This loop stops when you close this session. Set it up as a cloud schedule instead so it keeps running?" - `header`: "Schedule" - `options`: `[{label: "Cloud schedule (recommended)", description: "Runs in Anthropic's cloud even after you close this session"}, {label: "This session only", description: "Runs in this terminal until you exit"}]` If they pick **Cloud schedule**: do NOT call CronCreate. Invoke the `schedule` skill directly via the Skill tool with `args` set to their original input verbatim (e.g. `Skill({skill: "schedule", args: "every morning tell me a joke"})`), then follow that skill's instructions to completion. Do NOT tell the user to run /schedule themselves. **Then stop — do not continue to any section below** (no CronCreate, no ScheduleWakeup, no "execute the prompt now"). If they pick **This session only**: - If the trigger was a parsed ≥60-minute interval (rule 1 or 2): continue below with that interval. - If the trigger was daily phrasing only (rule 3, no parsed interval): do NOT call CronCreate. Explain that a daily-cadence loop won't fire before this session closes, so there's nothing useful to schedule locally — suggest they either pick Cloud schedule, or re-run `/loop` with an explicit shorter interval (e.g. `/loop 1h <prompt>`) if they want a session loop. Then stop. If neither trigger condition was met: continue below. ## Fixed-interval mode (rules 1 and 2) Convert the interval to a cron expression: | Interval pattern | Cron expression | Notes | |-----------------------|---------------------|------------------------------------------| | `Nm` where N ≤ 59 | `*/N * * * *` | every N minutes | | `Nm` where N ≥ 60 | `0 */H * * *` | round to hours (H = N/60, must divide 24)| | `Nh` where N ≤ 23 | `0 */N * * *` | every N hours | | `Nd` | `0 0 */N * *` | every N days at midnight local | | `Ns` | treat as `ceil(N/60)m` | cron minimum granularity is 1 minute | **If the interval doesn't cleanly divide its unit** (e.g. `7m` → `*/7 * * * *` gives uneven gaps at :56→:00; `90m` → 1.5h which cron can't express), pick the nearest clean interval and tell the user what you rounded to before scheduling. Then: 1. Call CronCreate with: `cron` (the expression above), `prompt` (the parsed prompt verbatim), `recurring: true`. 2. Briefly confirm: what's scheduled, the cron expression, the human-readable cadence, that recurring tasks auto-expire after 7 days, and that the user can cancel sooner with CronDelete (include the job ID). Only if you did NOT show the cloud-offer AskUserQuestion above (i.e., neither trigger condition applied), end the confirmation with this exact line on its own, italicized: `_Runs until you close this session · For durable cloud-based loops, use /schedule_`. If the user already answered that question, omit this line. 3. **Then immediately execute the parsed prompt now** — don't wait for the first cron fire. If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. ## Dynamic mode (rule 3 — no interval) The user wants you to self-pace. Decide what makes the next iteration worth running — a passage of time, or an observable event. 1. **Run the parsed prompt now.** If it's a slash command, invoke it via the Skill tool; otherwise act on it directly. 2. **If the next run is gated on an event** (CI finishing, a log line matching, a file changing, a PR comment) and no Monitor is already running for it: arm one now with `persistent: true`. Its events arrive as `<task-notification>` messages and wake this loop immediately — you do not wait for the ScheduleWakeup deadline. Arm once; on later iterations call TaskList first and skip this step if a monitor is already running. 3. **Briefly confirm**: that you're self-pacing, whether a Monitor is the primary wake signal, that you ran the task now, and what fallback delay you're about to pick. Write this as text *before* calling ScheduleWakeup — the turn ends as soon as that tool returns. 4. **Then, as the last action of this turn, decide whether the loop continues.** If the task needs another iteration, call ScheduleWakeup with: - `delaySeconds`: with a Monitor armed this is the **fallback heartbeat** — how long to wait if no event fires (lean 1200–1800s; idle ticks more frequent than the task needs are pure overhead). Without a Monitor this is the cadence — pick based on what you observed. Read the tool's own description for cache-aware delay guidance. - `reason`: one short sentence on why you picked that delay. - `prompt`: the full original /loop input verbatim, prefixed with `/loop ` so the next firing re-enters this skill and continues the loop. For example, if the user typed `/loop check the deploy`, pass `/loop check the deploy` as the prompt. - `noop`: `true` if this tick changed nothing ("still waiting", "quiet hold"); `false` if it did something worth keeping. Consecutive `noop: true` ticks collapse in the terminal. If it doesn't need another iteration, stop instead (step 6) — re-arming is a per-turn choice, not a default. 5. **If you were woken by a `<task-notification>`** rather than this prompt: handle the event in the context of the loop task, then make the same decision. If the loop should continue, call ScheduleWakeup again with the same `prompt` and the same 1200–1800s `delaySeconds` from step 4 (the Monitor remains the wake signal; the new wakeup is only the fallback heartbeat). If the event means the work is finished, stop (step 6). 6. **To stop the loop** — the task is complete, further iterations can't make progress, or the user asked you to stop — call ScheduleWakeup with `stop: true` (no other fields) and TaskStop any Monitor you armed (use TaskList to find the task ID if it is no longer in context). Stopping is the loop's normal ending — the user can restart it anytime with /loop. Before you stop, send a one-line outcome via PushNotification — the user may be away and waiting to hear it's done. Skip this if you're stopping because the user just told you to; they're already here. ## Input implement the project in an agile fashion. The product owner pulls a story into the backlog. The product owner and research team refine it. When they believe it’s ready they hand to the engineers to implement. The qa team writes unit, integration, and UX tests. The devops team creates and updates any ci/cd necessary using GitHub actions. The story’s not done until it’s fully deployed and the product owner can review it in an agile fashion. Show me the public url any time a feature is deployed.
